PlayStation Portable (PSP)
The PlayStation Portable (PSP), released by Sony in 2004, was a powerful handheld console with 3D graphics that rivaled the home consoles of its time. It offered a large library of games, including hits such as God of War: Chains of Olympus, Crisis Core: Final Fantasy VII, and Grand Theft Auto: Vice City Stories. In addition to games, the PSP supported movies, music and Internet access, making it a versatile entertainment device. With over 80 million units sold worldwide, it was a huge success.

Dreamcast
The Dreamcast, released by Sega in 1999, was the company's last home console. Despite its short life cycle, it is considered one of the most innovative consoles of its time. The Dreamcast was the first console with a built-in modem for online gaming and offered impressive graphics for its era. Titles such as Sonic Adventure, Shenmue and Jet Set Radio made the console a cult classic. Despite technical innovations, however, it was unable to compete with rivals such as the PlayStation 2.

PlayStation 1
The PlayStation 1 (PS1), released by Sony in 1994, marked the company's entry into the world of games consoles and revolutionized the industry with its CD-ROM technology. It offered groundbreaking 3D graphics and made longer, more complex games possible. Cult games such as Final Fantasy VII, Metal Gear Solid and Gran Turismo made the PS1 a worldwide success. With over 100 million units sold, it established Sony as one of the leading console manufacturers and laid the foundation for future PlayStation generations.

SFC (Super Famicom)
The Super Famicom (SFC for short), the Japanese version of the Super Nintendo Entertainment System (SNES), was released by Nintendo in Japan in 1990. It continued the success story of the Famicom and offered significantly improved graphics and sound quality thanks to its 16-bit technology. Games such as Super Mario World, The Legend of Zelda: A Link to the Past and Super Metroid shaped the era and made the Super Famicom one of the most influential consoles of the 1990s. Today, it is considered a classic in video game history.

Famicom
The Famicom (short for "Family Computer") was released by Nintendo in Japan in 1983 and is the Japanese version of the Nintendo Entertainment System (NES). It revolutionized the gaming industry with its 8-bit technology and spawned iconic games such as Super Mario Bros, The Legend of Zelda and Donkey Kong. The Famicom was the beginning of Nintendo's success in the console world and laid the foundation for many of the most popular franchises that still exist today.

Super Nintendo (SNES)
The Super Nintendo Entertainment System (SNES) was released by Nintendo in 1990 as the successor to the Famicom. It was based on 16-bit technology and offered significantly improved graphics and sound quality compared to its predecessor. With legendary games such as Super Mario World, The Legend of Zelda: A Link to the Past, and Super Metroid, the SNES made its mark on 90s video game culture. It enabled more complex gameplay mechanics and established Nintendo as the leading console manufacturer. Today, the SNES is considered one of the best consoles in video game history.

Arcade Games
Arcade games are video games that are played in special machines in public arcades. They peaked in the 70s and 80s with classics such as Pac-Man, Space Invaders, and Street Fighter II. The machines were often coin-operated and offered intense, fast-paced gaming experiences. Arcade games were technological pioneers in video game development and shaped many popular genres such as shooters, fighting games and racing games. Today, they are particularly popular with retro gaming fans and collectors.

Capcom Play System I (CPS1)
The Capcom Play System 1 (CPS1) is an arcade platform developed by Capcom in 1988 and based on powerful 16-bit technology. It became particularly well known for iconic games such as Street Fighter II, Final Fight and Ghouls 'n Ghosts, which shaped the arcade scene of the late 80s and early 90s. The CPS1 hardware allowed for more detailed graphics and smoother animations, making it one of the most influential arcade platforms of the time. Capcom used it to establish numerous successful game series.

Capcom Play System II (CPS2)
The Capcom Play System II (CPS2) is an arcade hardware platform introduced by Capcom in 1993. It built on the success of the CPS1 and allowed for more technically advanced games thanks to better graphics and sound capabilities. Many famous games were released on the CPS2, including Street Fighter Alpha, Marvel vs. Capcom, and Alien vs. Predator. The CPS2 utilized interchangeable modules installed on the motherboard and was known for many classic fighting and shoot-'em-up games. It was used until the late 1990s.

Gameboy Advance
The Game Boy Advance (GBA) was released by Nintendo in 2001 and was the successor to the Game Boy Color. The GBA offered 32-bit graphics on a handheld for the first time, which made it possible to play significantly more complex games than on previous devices. Games such as The Legend of Zelda: The Minish Cap, Metroid Fusion and Pokémon Ruby & Sapphire became classics on the console. The GBA was backwards compatible with Game Boy and Game Boy Color games, making it a versatile console for retro gaming fans.

Gameboy Color
The Game Boy Color (GBC) was released by Nintendo in 1998 as the successor to the original Game Boy. The main difference was the color screen, which displayed games in a wide range of colors. The GBC was backwards compatible so that all Game Boy games could still be played. Popular titles such as Pokémon Gold & Silver, The Legend of Zelda: Oracle of Ages/Seasons and Super Mario Bros Deluxe made the console very successful. The Game Boy Color was a bridge between the classic Game Boy and more modern handheld devices.

Sega Genesis
The Sega Genesis, also known as the Mega Drive, was released in Japan in 1988 and in the USA in 1989. It was Sega's most successful console and competed against Nintendo's Super Nintendo. The Genesis featured 16-bit graphics and an impressive game library with titles such as Sonic the Hedgehog, Streets of Rage and Golden Axe. The console achieved cult status thanks to its fast-paced, action-packed games and attracted a large fan base, particularly in the US. The Genesis was a key factor in the 'console wars' of the 90s.

Mega Drive
The Mega Drive is the version of the Sega Genesis released in Europe and Japan, which came onto the market in Japan in 1988 and in the USA in 1989. With its 16-bit hardware, the Mega Drive offered impressive graphics and sound performance for the time. It was particularly well known for games such as Sonic the Hedgehog, Streets of Rage and Altered Beast. The Mega Drive became an important competitor for Nintendo's Super Nintendo and contributed significantly to the console wars of the 90s.

Nintendo 64
The Nintendo 64 (N64) was released by Nintendo in 1996 and was the company's first console with 64-bit technology that enabled 3D graphics. The N64 is known for legendary games such as Super Mario 64, The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time and GoldenEye 007, and its innovative controller with an analog stick was revolutionary for controlling 3D games. Despite competition from Sony PlayStation and Sega Saturn, the N64 remains one of the most influential consoles in video game history.

Sega NAOMI
The Sega NAOMI (New Arcade Operation Machine Idea) was an arcade platform released in 1998 and based on the hardware of the Dreamcast console. It was known for its performance and expanded memory capacity, which allowed for larger and more complex games. Many popular arcade games such as Marvel vs. Capcom 2, Virtua Tennis and Crazy Taxi were developed on the NAOMI hardware. The platform was very popular with arcade operators due to its cost efficiency and longevity and contributed to Sega's success in arcades.

1. Capcom vs. SNK: Millennium Fight 2000

7. Street Fighter Zero 3 Upper

Nintendo DS
The Nintendo DS was released by Nintendo in 2004 and was one of the first handheld consoles to introduce a dual screen, one of which was a touchscreen. This innovative approach enabled new game mechanics and was groundbreaking for mobile gaming. Games such as New Super Mario Bros, Mario Kart DS and The Legend of Zelda: Phantom Hourglass became popular on the DS. The DS was also backwards compatible with Game Boy Advance games, which added to its appeal. With over 150 million units sold, it was extremely successful.

NeoGeo
The NeoGeo is a game console developed by SNK that was launched in 1990 and was used as both an arcade system and a home console. It was known for its powerful hardware and enabled arcade quality at home. Famous games such as Metal Slug, The King of Fighters, and Samurai Shodown made the NeoGeo platform legendary. Despite its high price and rather exclusive user base, the NeoGeo is now considered a cult console, especially among retro gaming enthusiasts.

NeoGeo Pocket
The Neo Geo Pocket was released by SNK in 1998 and was a handheld designed specifically for retro gaming. It featured monochrome graphics and a small but iconic game library. Its successor, the Neo Geo Pocket Color (1999), brought color support and expanded the game selection with titles like SNK vs. Capcom: The Match of the Millennium and Metal Slug: 1st Mission. Although the Neo Geo Pocket was not as successful as its competitors, it gained a loyal fan base and remains a popular device among collectors.

NeoGeo Pocket Color
The Neo Geo Pocket Color (NGPC) was released by SNK in 1999 as the successor to the monochrome Neo Geo Pocket. It offered a color palette and was technically capable of supporting arcade-style games in handheld format. The NGPC received praise for its long-lasting battery and a game library featuring titles such as SNK vs. Capcom: The Match of the Millennium, Metal Slug: 1st Mission, and Samurai Shodown! Despite positive reviews and innovative features, its success lagged behind the Game Boy Color. Today, the NGPC is popular with retro collectors.

PC Engine
The PC Engine, known as TurboGrafx-16 in the USA, was developed by NEC and Hudson Soft in 1987. It was an 8-bit console that came onto the market with an advanced graphics engine and many popular games. The console became famous for its shooter games such as Blazing Lazers and R-Type as well as role-playing games such as Ys I & II. The PC Engine was also the first console with a CD-ROM drive, making it particularly innovative for its time.

Nintendo Entertainment System (NES)
The Nintendo Entertainment System (NES), which was released in Japan in 1983 as the Famicom (Family Computer), was one of the most successful home consoles of the 1980s and made a significant contribution to the revival of the video game industry. With iconic games such as Super Mario Bros, The Legend of Zelda, and Metroid, the NES laid the foundation for many popular Nintendo franchises. It was known for its 8-bit graphics and offered gamers a wide range of genres, from platform games to role-playing games.
